S.J. Bennett on the brilliance of 1960s British mysteries and thrillers

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

My first date with my husband, twenty-five years ago, was a series of tests. Do you like Blackadder, and agree that series 4 is the best? Are you a fan of Bladerunner? The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy? And, very importantly, did you read Len Deighton as a teenager? Luckily for our future son, we both passed.

Deighton’s unpredictable protagonists, and his propulsive writing style, were a revelation to sixteen year-old me. I always think of his heroes in cars, on the run from something awful, on the way to something worse.

Growing up in the 80s, I was a fan of the classics, of P.G. Wodehouse and Jilly Cooper. People behaved badly, very badly sometimes, but their role in the moral universe was clear. Then, in books written at around the time I was born, as the dust was finally settling from WW II, I found myself in the cynical, murky world of double agents and double-crossing, inspired in part by the Noir of the 30s, but updated to the Cold War: we can’t trust the men in charge, and the world may be about to end.

In the historical series that I’m writing featuring the late Queen, I’ve reached the mid-60s. The last book was set at the height of the space race in 1961, and the new one takes place in the Caribbean in 1966. I think of this as a sunny highlight: the Swinging Sixties, The Beatles, Carnaby Street, the young Rolling Stones….

But research quickly reminded me that it was the time of Vietnam, the Bay of Pigs, the call for independence among many of the UK’s old colonies, of drugs and disillusion, fear and paranoia. This is the world that Deighton explored in Funeral in Berlin.

His unnamed protagonist, played by Michael Caine and given the name Harry Palmer in the films (because it was the most boring one they could jointly come up with), had already had two outings, in The IPCRESS File and Horse Under Water. Both are a bit bonkers. Funeral in Berlin, published in 1964, was where Deighton really got into his stride.

The Berlin Wall had only gone up three years before. I lived there in the 80s, when the Wall went round the back of our garden and there was a Russian sentry post a few meters beyond. People were still dying in the attempt to get across, but by then, the Wall had its own mythology.

When Deighton was writing, the weird cruelty of streets and even houses being cut in half by barbed wire and concrete was still fresh, and bled into the text. I equally loved John Le Carré for his more cerebral evocation of the times, but Deighton’s working-class anti-hero, at odds with the Establishment and unwilling to look away, was the one who really got me into the genre.

How does Frederick Forsyth get away with the plot of The Day of the Jackal? Set in 1962, in the aftermath of the Algerian war of independence, it’s a story in which we already know the outcome—the assassin must surely fail to kill Charles de Gaulle, who didn’t die until 1970—and yet we’re gripped as we watch him regularly outwit the authorities, who are no slouches in tracking him down.

Inserting a false narrative into real events is what I do with my own series, so I’m with him as Forsyth tightly interweaves the two. I always used to feel that the final-final twist, where a character we think is dead shows up unexpectedly, didn’t quite make sense. However, when I recently relistened to the audiobook version, I realized that it had been set up that way all along. It makes the reader completely rethink the middle section, paying tribute to the value of dogged police work, even when the odds against success seem overwhelming.

The novel also pits a middle-class hero, the police deputy commissioner Claude Lebel, against a dismissive, upper-class superior. The moment when Lebel gets his revenge is very satisfying. Like Deighton, Forsyth was no fan of the old-school ruling classes, although he had an obvious respect for methodical assassins.

James Bond (another iconic spy name designed to be as boring as possible) evolved gradually as a character. He was based in part on old Etonian Ian Fleming himself, partly on some of the men he encountered in wartime naval intelligence, and later on the director of Dr No on screen, the refined and sophisticated Terence Young, It was Young who gave him his sharp tailoring from Anthony Sinclair, his taste for martinis and elegant way with cigarettes.

But the first man to play him, with a panther-like grace that got him the part, grew up in a tenement in Edinburgh, leaving home to work as a bricklayer, lorry driver and coffin polisher, among other things. Would Bond have been as interesting if he hadn’t had the magnetism that Sean Connery brought to the role? At a time of great social mobility, he was a stand-out star.

Connery didn’t play Bond in the film of On Her Majesty’s Secret Service. By 1969, he’d given up on the carnival of publicity around his life. But he was filming Dr No near Fleming’s Goldeneye estate in 1962 when Fleming wrote the novel.

Fleming probably had Connery (and those Anthony Sinclair suits) in mind for the Bond who pretends to be Sir Hilary Bray from the London College of Arms. In the end, though, the part went to the actor George Lazenby, who made the somewhat premature decision that James Bond would be a busted flush in the 1970s, and turned down a chance to return.

In this book, Blofeld is the villain, and the plot moves on from classic Cold War territory to a more generalized tech-driven anxiety, in which a bio-war is about to be unleashed by unsuspecting female debutantes. It’s not the most feminist of plots, but I love the novel for Bond’s female companion, Tracy di Vicenzo, the only woman in the Bond canon—apart from Vesper Lynd—who I think holds her own in a three-dimensional way.

Played to perfection by Diana Rigg onscreen, Tracy is smart, well-connected (her father is a very serious crime boss), reckless, resourceful, quick-thinking and athletic. Having created a woman who’s a worthy match for Bond, the series obviously couldn’t keep her. The ending of is so tragic and unexpected that it’s still gut-wrenching, even now.

On the whole, I’d say we don’t think of Agatha Christie when we think of the 1960s. The perennial films and TV series conjure inter-war fashions, Miss Marple’s Edwardian correctness, old-fashioned country houses, and the Art Deco smartness of Hercule Poirot.

But her success spanned decades. The Pale Horse, published in 1961, was partly inspired by the very popular novels by Dennis Wheatley, which often featured the supernatural. In Christie’s version we have foggy nights, witches, deserted country lanes and several predicted deaths in seemingly-impossible circumstances.

One of the secrets of Christie’s success was how cutting-edge she was with her murder methods. She loved to know about new poisons and the latest gadgets. Think of the role a Dictaphone plays in one of her most famous early books—Dictaphone as a company had only been created three years before it was published in the 1920s.

And true to the Christie formula, the solution to the Pale Horse mystery contains an up-to-date knowledge of chemistry. It works both as an out-of-time, spooky novel and a contemporary, 1960s plot.

In my books, Queen Elizabeth II occasionally has a Dick Francis novel at her bedside, and she was a big fan in real life. As a jockey, Francis was closely connected with the royal family. He was riding the Queen Mother’s horse, Devon Loch, towards victory in the 1956 Grand National, when the horse inexplicably fell. This was when he retired to become a writer.

Dead Cert, which came out in 1962, was his first novel. It’s centered around great horses falling in important races, and Francis’s visceral first-hand experience lights up the page. But there’s more to his books than horses. As Britain’s post-war economy took off, he branched out with his wife, Mary, to research a wide range of industries. exploring the glamour and danger of his age.

Like Deighton’s, I read his books in the 1980s, when Proof grabbed my attention. I’m drawn to Francis’s vulnerable heroes, so gentle with horses, so outrageously lucky or unlucky with women, so tough at withstanding injuries of all kinds (as the author was) and beatings from his sadistic villains. I have to read through half-closed eyelids, but still, I have to read on.

One of the things I like about Dick Francis’ career is that it took time to take off. Like Ian Fleming’s, his early books sold well enough, but there weren’t any fireworks. It was only as the series progressed that publicity grew, and he became the crimewriting juggernaut his estate still is today. There’s a lesson for publishers there, perhaps: have faith in your writers. Stick with them. Fandom comes with time and repetition.

It’s a lesson another British spy novelist embodies. Mick Herron’s Slough House books languished in the also-rans for years, until a US publisher spotted them and they got a new lease of life.

This autumn, season 6 of Slow Horses returns triumphantly to our screens. It contains many of the tropes of its 1960s predecessors: downtrodden heroes, defying the odds and besting upper class antagonists; realistically dingy streets and double-crossing; up-to-date technology and an overarching sense of anxiety. We can’t trust the men in charge, and the world may be about to end. Plus ça change.

S. J. Bennett was born in Yorkshire, England, and lives in London. An army child, she grew up traveling around the world. Her first novel was published when she was forty-two, after a varied career and lots of procrastination. She is the award-winning author of several books for children and teaches and podcasts about writing.

Kennedy Cole on writing about race through invasion horror and parasitism

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This essay will break down the themes of racism, homophobia, and existing as other that I touch on in There Used to Be People Here. I’ll expand on my thought process behind Samuel’s dilemma with the taur, and explore some of the complex moments that happen in the book (as much I can without spoilers). Imposter syndrome and belonging are two of Samuel’s biggest struggles; I plan to touch on that in aspects of my own life, and as an issue Black people deal with as a whole.

Foreign occupation of the body has always been a particularly terrifying horror element to me. The idea of losing control of not just our mind, but our being—autonomy, duality, and person—sounds so far out there that to some, it might be too impossible to fear.

Of course, it is an impossibility, in some ways. Very rarely, if ever, does one quite literally lose control of themselves to an outside source. In some ways, though, the idea is very real.

Take imposter syndrome, for example, the intrusive thought that you as an individual don’t belong in a room you’ve been—presumably—welcomed into. It happens to all of us, doesn’t it? We question our ability to be, wonder whether or not we truly belong in a space we, more often than not, do.

We allow, then, ourselves to change. We lose ourselves to paranoia. We become invisible in a space that originally valued us because of our unique ability to shine.

In my debut, There Used to Be People Here, Samuel Barkley is the only Black detective in a small police department in Mississippi, 1978. He works alone by choice, but also by default. He consistently questions his own worth, mostly as a result of the constant stares and pressured environment created by the officers around him, but also as a result of his own deeply buried trauma. Things shift when he’s introduced to a new case—a strange murder that starts in a gay bar—and an odd new partner who he has uncertain feelings about, Harvey Wilkins.

And of course Samuel wonders, do I deserve this?

As a Black woman, I’ve often asked this question myself whenever I find I’ve been given new, and most often exciting, opportunities. I attended a predominantly white K-12 school from Kindergarten until graduation, so trust me when I say I’ve had my fair share of stares and pressure and deeply buried trauma.

I’ve also had privilege: the privilege to learn at a private institution, to explore creative writing in a passionate environment, to research subjects I love and be encouraged to grow. At every turn, though, I found myself questioning my (what I now know is natural) ability to do so.

I found myself questioning my Blackness, because many other people around me—both Black and white—were doing the same. Was I truly talented, or was it a case of borrowed opportunity? Were my dreams tangible, or faraway wonders? Could I truly achieve all that I set my mind to, or was I just the only Black girl pretending to be white?

The taur—the worm-like, parasitic hive-mind antagonizing Wenton, Mississippi in my debut—propose this question in the most fitting way possible: by occupying the body. They feed on Samuel’s twisted desire to be invisible by creating an environment where this is true. To Samuel, the taur’s true motivations are murky, but their plans are clear: to make everyone, everywhere the same.

The idea of sameness can be beguiling to those of us who face discrimination because of our innate, unchangeable qualities. Samuel’s position as a detective in the Wenton Police Department is earned, and yet his role as the only Black man in the room pushes him to call his sense of self into question, much like I did as the only Black girl in my grade for thirteen years. It’s easy to wish ourselves away, to wonder whether homogeneity would erase the questions and stares altogether and allow our inner selves to shine.

And though in theory it would, our innate, unchangeable qualities are lost as a result. Our Blackness—which, even in a room full of people who are not, does matter—becomes invisible. We forfeit our bodies. We allow the parasite within us to win.

Throughout the story, Samuel’s autonomy does become forfeit, not because of a parasitic entity infecting him, but because of his own fear of existing as other. His queerness, which he discovers through his unique partnership with Harvey, is something he attempts to fold away for later. Over time, Samuel’s work as a detective shifts from a childhood dream to a tool determining his self worth. Working his way up through the ranks is his only true talent. And to Samuel, his Blackness is only an obstacle.

When I first began writing There Used to Be People Here, my identity had yet to be fully determined. In many ways, it still is wholly undiscovered. I’m a twenty-three year old woman publishing my very first book, preparing to be married to the love of my life in just under a year, and still looking for a job in my field long after graduating with a bachelor’s degree. I’m living this life for the first time, taking it one day at a time just like everyone else.

My sense of self, though, is much stronger than it was when I started my debut. I can think of no better reason for this than Samuel.

I parallel my novel’s main character in more ways than one. What frightens him frightens me. As Samuel works through his fear of unifomity—and the temptation to try it for himself—I was finding my voice as a queer Black writer.

To be blunt, writing about my race has been difficult for me. Up until this point, I’d never centered any of my stories around a Black main character, let alone a Black man. I wouldn’t realize it until I was well into drafting, but writing There Used to Be People Here challenged everything I’d learned about what it means to be a writer. My own work forced me to face the pattern I’d perpetuated, and in the context of the story, forced Samuel to look inward and face himself.

Now, I can look back and map Samuel’s journey alongside my own. Our Blackness—our innate, unchangeable worth—destines us to be othered. So, we shine not in spite of it, but because of it. We choose diversity, not because we welcome discrimination, but because our differences are a direct reflection of our autonomy. We reject the parasite and embrace each other, because our worth is determined by the depth of our love.

Kennedy Cole is a speculative author with a BFA in Creative Writing and a Certificate in Publishing. Born in North Carolina, she lives by the beach with her partner and enjoys playing games, puzzling, sipping matcha, and collecting tattoos. Her creative portfolio includes an array of published fiction, poetry, and nonfiction with Oakland Arts Review, Carolina Muse, Second Story Journal, and others. There Used to Be People Here is her first novel.

Seeking Justice in Deep South Georgia

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

I had finished the third run-through of my closing argument, so I went down to the pond to see how John Petrey, my chief assistant district attorney, was doing with his portion.

I walked slowly and quietly so as not to disturb him. As I drew near, I caught sight of him standing on the dock, facing the water in his T-shirt, shorts, and Birkenstocks. Tall and slim, glasses perched on the end of his nose, hair a little disheveled, he was delivering a persuasive argument to what appeared to be an empty pond.

Across from the dock, though, I spotted the gray snout and black eyes of an alligator, the rest of the animal still and immersed in the water. The animal was watching him. Because the sun was shining from that direction, the light combined with Petrey’s terrible eyesight caused him not to see it.

That old gator was the most attentive juror he ever addressed.

We were two hundred miles from where the murder happened, living in a hunting lodge outside Albany, Georgia, preparing to close the biggest case I would ever try.

Eighteen months earlier, on December 15, 2000, during a cold and rainy night in Georgia, Derwin Brown, the sheriff-elect of DeKalb County (one of the largest metropolitan Atlanta counties), was ambushed and shot twelve times in his own driveway. The killing sent shockwaves through the Atlanta area and Georgia and was reported nationally and even internationally. The preceding summer, Brown defeated the incumbent sheriff in a highly contentious campaign that was focused on corruption allegations against the sitting sheriff. An investigative reporter for a local television station exposed shocking details of the corruption. Brown was three days from being sworn in. His family was inside the house waiting for him.

Derwin’s wife ordered a custom-tailored sheriff’s uniform for the swearing-in. She had hung it inside the storm door so it would be the first thing he saw when he entered the home.

At the morgue that night, I noticed his wedding ring had been flattened. A bullet had struck it. He had apparently been holding his hand up to defend himself.

As the district attorney, I had a policy that my team would show up any time an officer was killed in the line of duty, which is how I found myself alone with the bullet-ravaged body of Sheriff-elect Brown for a few minutes. I knew that if we found the killer(s), I would be the prosecutor.

The trial was not held in DeKalb County. There had been too much media coverage. The judge granted the defense request to move the trial.

The prosecution and the defense each got to suggest three cities. One of mine was Savannah. If I was going to spend eight weeks somewhere, I thought it would be nice to be near the ocean.

People who hear “change of venue” may think of it as a technicality, or as a strategy of defense attorneys to seek out a different jury pool. It should be neither. It occurs because a judge in the county where the crime occurred determines the people who live there are not impartial, and the case needs to be moved to another county to ensure justice is delivered.

When the order came in, I thought Petrey was playing a joke on me when he announced the case would be tried in Albany, the county seat of Dougherty County, in deep South Georgia. My hometown. I have three generations buried in that sandy soil below the gnat line in Georgia.

A few years before the trial, a hundred-year flood caused the Flint River to flood over the cemetery. The caskets were hermetically sealed, so when the flood water reached them they shot up out of the ground like missiles. Fred Mays, a GBI agent and one of the most important people on my prosecution team, once told me there were two things in this world he was afraid of: rattlesnakes and dead people. During that flood, Fred’s job was to take a motorboat out over the cemetery and lasso the caskets and bring them back.

My grandmother was buried in one of the caskets that floated down the Flint River. I always wondered if Fred was the one who returned her casket to the cemetery.

The trial was held in the county courthouse where I had been sworn in as a young lawyer, and where the state of Georgia once prosecuted Martin Luther King Jr. I watched Dr. King march down Slappey Drive when I was a boy. I stood on the sidewalk with Sarah, who cared for my sister and me, and I had no idea what I was looking at. I thought it was a parade.

Forty years later, I stood in that courtroom and quoted Dr. King to a jury.

The hunting lodge was the only place we could find in the Albany area to house our team of lawyers and investigators without interruption for two months. It had a cabin that looked like the one in the old Snuffy Smith cartoon. Seventeen miles outside town, it had a tin roof, a ladder up to a sleeping room with windows on every wall, and the pond. No phones. No television. Other small cabins were also on the grounds of the hunting lodge. This is what the biggest corruption and murder prosecution in the history of the state looked like from the inside. Lawyers in shorts, isolated from the outside world, arguing to the woods.

Months before, a lawyer who had worked in my office as an assistant district attorney came to my house.

He told me not to ask him any questions. He said he knew I did not carry a gun, but I needed to start carrying one, and I needed round-the-clock security.

Lawyers are allowed to break client confidentiality when a life is in danger. My name was on a hit list.

In Georgia, as in almost all states, the district attorney is elected and answers only to the people he or she serves. (This is in contrast to the federal system, where the United States Attorneys are under the supervision of the Attorney General who serves at the pleasure of the President.) It was my decision, and my decision alone, whether to bring charges against those whom I believed were responsible for Brown’s assassination. After sharing the evidence with the most experienced lawyers on my staff and seeking their input, Tom Clegg, my chief trial assistant, said, “You are the boss. It’s your decision.” Attorney General Robert Jackson, who was later appointed to the U.S. Supreme Court, said in a speech in 1940, “The prosecutor has more control over life, liberty, and reputation than any other person in America.”

A fair question is why I decided to be the lead prosecutor on this case. First, I was a career prosecutor who had tried over 100 jury trials before being elected district attorney. In many district attorney offices, especially in urban areas, the district attorney is primarily an administrator and rarely goes to court.  Some elected district attorneys have never tried a felony case, either as a prosecutor or defense attorney. The courtroom was my sandbox.

Second, the ultimate responsibility for a conviction or an acquittal would rest on my shoulders as the district attorney. I wanted to be in charge if I was to bear the responsibility for a win or a loss. Also, I had some of the best and most experienced trial lawyers in the country assisting me. I was not alone.

Finally, this case was critically important to the people I was elected to serve. A sheriff-elect was murdered before he could take the oath of office. The voters of one of the largest urban counties in the country had their vote taken away from them before the candidate of their choosing could serve. Our investigation revealed a pattern of corruption on a scale that none of us could have imagined before the murder.

As I put pen to paper to write the story of this case in A Rainy Night in Georgia (Prometheus/Simon & Schuster), I was struck by how the issues in this case remain relevant and timely today. The strength of our criminal justice system depends on having people who are committed to public service, integrity, and adherence to the rule of law.

Petrey gave his part of the closing argument to a jury of twelve. (It was a while before he learned about the juror with a snout.)

When the verdict came in, a reporter I had known for years, the one who had helped break this case in the first place, asked me what I was going to do next. I told him I was getting a six-pack, my Jimmy Buffett CDs, and my boom box, and I was going to the beach.

We chose Bermuda. My family appreciated being out of the public eye, on an island with some of the strictest firearms laws in the world.

J.Tom Morgan served as the elected District Attorney of DeKalb County, GA, from 1992-2004. He was the first recipient from the United States to be awarded the Special Achievement Award from the International Association of Prosecutors in 2003. Prior to his time as DA, Morgan served as an Assistant District Attorney in DeKalb County for nine years and was the first prosecutor in Georgia--and one of the first in the nation--to specialize in the prosecution of crimes against children. As a nationally recognized expert on criminal justice, he has appeared on The Oprah Winfrey Show, The Today Show, CNN, Court TV, and 48 Hours, among others. Morgan has lectured to prosecutors and law enforcement officials in more than 30 states, Puerto Rico, and Europe, and served on the faculty of the National College of District Attorneys.

The Sky Today on Thursday, November 5: Smoke out the Southern Cigar

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Looking for a sky event this week? Check out our full Sky This Week column.

Tonight’s target is a bit of a test: How far south can you see? At a latitude of 40° north, NGC 55 in Sculptor — also called the Southern Cigar Galaxy or the String of Pearls Galaxy — only rises about 10° above the southern horizon at its highest, which occurs around 9 P.M. local time. Observers farther south will see it higher in the sky, while those farther north will see it closer to the horizon or be unable to make it out at all.

For those observers who can catch it, NGC 55 is a bright, 8th-magnitude edge-on barred spiral located just under 4° northwest of magnitude 2.4 Alpha (α) Phoenicis in Phoenix, which sits below the galaxy as you look south around this time of night. This galaxy is not only bright, it’s quite large, stretching roughly the width of the Full Moon (½°) at its longest. It is quite thin, however, only some 6’ wide along the other dimension. You’ll immediately see that its nucleus is not in the center, but offset to the west. Several other bright knots of star formation may also be visible within the galaxy’s plane — particularly if you drop in a nebula filter.

Sunrise: 6:34 A.M.Sunset: 4:53 P.M.Moonrise: 2:50 A.M.Moonset: 2:53 P.M.Moon Phase: Waning crescent (10%)*Times for sunrise, sunset, moonrise, and moonset are given in local time from 40° N 90° W. The Moon’s illumination is given at 10 P.M. local time from the same location.

Alison Klesman is senior editor of Astronomy magazine. She holds a Ph.D. in astronomy and has studied a variety of topics, from minor planets to supermassive black holes.

Animal Attack Roundup, Nov. 4

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

While they predominate the media reports, most animal attack victims are not tourists unwisely attempting to take selfies with wildlife. People who live and work every day among animals are the ones who most often face the consequences.

As expanding populations push human settlements further into wildlife habitat, we can expect to see more conflicts. Here are some recent incidents.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Tisha Mukherjee, CC BY-SA 4.0

In Indonesia, 24-year-old Selviana Ambu Kaka was attacked by a crocodile while washing clothes in the Bondo Kodi River. Her fiancé and his sister witnessed a large crocodile emerging from the water, biting Ambu Kaka on the leg, and dragging her into the river. They immediately alerted nearby relatives and authorities, and a search commenced.

The people of Southwest Sumba, the closest town, organized and combed the area with nets. When they found a large crocodile matching witness descriptions, they hauled it onto shore and killed it. The animal measured nearly 4.9 meters long.

Early on the morning of October 24, Ambu Kaka’s remains were found approximately 30m from the attack site. She is one of many such victims in Indonesia, which sees more saltwater crocodile attacks than any other country.

Habitat destruction is largely behind the massive number of human-crocodile conflicts. It’s an understandably hard sell, however, for conservation workers to convince locals that preventing attacks means protecting the perpetrators.

The victim had recently returned from Jakarta to become formally engaged on October 30.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/ Rohit Gangwal, CC BY-SA 4.0

A 65-year-old farm laborer, Tulsi Rama Sawar, was killed by a leopard in state of Maharashtra, just north of Mumbai, on November 1. The noise of the attack summoned help from the locals. By the time they arrived, the leopard had fled, but Sawar was deceased. Her case comes less than a month after a leopard killed a 52-year-old woman in the same area.

Town residents are demanding immediate government action to capture the animal. If their demands for cage traps and cameras are not granted, locals threaten collective protest. The attacks represent a threat to their lives, but also their livelihoods, as farmers are unable to work the fields out of fear of attack.

Elsewhere, in Uttar Pradesh near the Nepal border, a 45-year-old farmer named Kennu fought off an attacking leopard, sustaining serious injuries. The man was working in a sugarcane field when the predator leaped out from hiding. Kennu fought back, and the animal retreated long enough for him to flee home, where family members brought him to medical care. Forestry officials have urged agricultural workers to avoid working in the fields alone.

Farm workers are not the only leopard victims, however. Early in the morning of November 4, a 15-year-old girl was dragged from her home in the Shahdol district of central India.

Saraswati Singh Gond was sleeping in bed beside her sister on their veranda, which did not have a door. A leopard entered during the night, not even waking her sister. Singh Gond’s body was found approximately 300m from the house. The search for the leopard continues.

Photo: Wikimedia Commons/Shadow Ayush, CC BY-SA 4.0

Leopards are only one of the wildlife threats that rural Indians face. A 55-year-old woman was gathering mushrooms in Utterakand in northern India on October 30 when she was attacked and killed by a wild elephant. Devaki Rana was lifted up and thrown to the ground, her son reported.

The incident occurred in one of India’s elephant corridors, which allow highly endangered Asian elephant herds to move between their fragmented habitats. The corridors are essential to conservation, but also present a concentrated area where attacks are more common.

Another fatal encounter took place in the state of Chhattisgarh in east-central India. A man of between 35 and 40 years of age, traveling by motorcycle with his nephew, came upon an elephant standing in the road. It immediately charged them, killing the uncle, while his nephew managed to hide beneath a parked truck. Eventually, the elephant left, and he was able to escape.

Locals believe that the elephant in question is one known as Kabra Kaan, which they see frequently and which has been connected with earlier incidents. The Forestry Department, however, has not confirmed this.

Elephant corridors allow them to pass between the remaining forested areas. Photo: Wildlife Trust of India

In Japan, brown bear attacks have been rising for several years. As in many places, human settlements are encroaching on brown bear territory. A decrease in farmland has left no boundary between the urban and the forested worlds. The government has responded with controversial bear culls, despite the concerns of conservation workers.

The latest incident is very different from the increasingly common attack stories. Late on the night of October 30, an express train running on Hokkaido’s Soya Line collided with a large brown bear. The body of the bear was trapped under the train, and the wheels derailed.

A brown bear became trapped beneath the wheels of Sarobetsu No 3. Photo: Hokkaido Railway Company

None of the crew or 32 passengers were injured in the crash. However, fearing more bears were in the area, officials kept everyone on the train overnight, until they could be safely extracted the following morning. Company officials have confirmed that this is the first bear-collision derailment in their nearly 40-year history.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.

Catch Electronics Shock and Drop Failures Before Hardware Is Built

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Not registered yet? Click here to register now.

Already registered? Click here to log in to the hub.

First register your details to create a user profile for the hub, then login to access all content within the hub.

Already registered? Click here to log in to the hub.

Electronic products now pack circuits, structures, thermal paths, and packaging into very small spaces. These physical domains interact with one another. A packaging choice can change how a shock load reaches a circuit board. Such interactions are hard to predict. Most are found during physical qualification, late in development. At that stage, the design is mostly fixed. A failure leads to redesign, rebuilds, and retesting. Physical tests give strong evidence of robustness, but they give limited insight into load paths and failure margins. Traditional simulation gives more detail, but it needs specialist skills and long manual setup. This white paper explains how guided virtual validation brings shock and drop assessment into the design phase. It reviews common test configurations, model inputs, and acceptance criteria. A packaged drop case study then shows how packaging can be optimized across all drop orientations and checked against physical test results.

Winter Container Designs from Ellen and Tracy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Hi everyone! I hope you’re enjoying the cool weather and a nice cup of coffee—or seven. With winter right around the corner, I’ve been absolutely gushing over these container designs from New Jersey garden designers Ellen Goldfarb and Tracy Buttrick of What’s Blooming. The wheels are turning! Here’s another round of their winter container designs to inspire us.

These sturdy, no-nonsense planters stand guard on either side of an impressive entry in Saddle River, New Jersey. The challenge here is matching the weight of the pots and doors. We had to carefully avoid the glass lamps just above them.

We carefully placed birch logs and then filled around them with some golden curly willow (Salix matsudana ‘Tortuosa’, Zones 4–8) and berried Chinese juniper (Juniperus chinensis, Zones 4–9). The red berries are winterberry (Ilex verticillata, Zones 3–9). Greens are a bit of desert juniper (Juniperus osteosperma, Zones 3–9), noble fir (Abies procera, Zones 5–6), and incense cedar (Calocedrus decurrens, Zones 5–8). We also used frosted pine cones, some lotus pods (Nelumbo cvs., Zones 4–10), and silver brunia.

Close-up of one of the planters in the previous picture

Another pot at the same house in Saddle River: This one sits at the front door, where we had the same challenge. We were working with chunky planters, limited porch space, impressive doors, and breakable lamps above.

We opted for thick bunches of redtwig dogwood (Cornus sericea cvs., Zones 2–7) against the off-white stone walls. We added lots of desert juniper, red winterberries, natural brown pine cones, some silver bell eucalyptus, noble fir, and incense cedar.

This pot and its twin on the other side of the door and landing stand in front of the one in the previous picture, but down one step. You can see a peek of the red branches in the background.

For contrast with the red planter in back, we used cooler colors: yellow twig dogwood (Cornus sericea ‘Flaviramea’, Zones 3–7), artificial white berries, and frosted pine cones. Finally, we added clusters of silver brunia, noble fir branches, and incense cedar.

A small planter on a covered porch in Saddle River, New Jersey, is crowned with southern magnolia (Magnolia grandiflora, Zones 6–10) leaves. Pieces of incense cedar peek through here and there. We love using seeded eucalyptus, and that year we found really pretty purple branches of it. Some natural brown pine cones and clusters of silver brunia complete the planting.

The tall branches are white-painted “sweet huck” branches (huckleberry) mixed with dark redtwig dogwood. Under them are branches of seeded eucalyptus and red winterberries, some frosted pine cones, a little noble fir, and incense cedar.

Thanks for another dose of winter container inspiration, Ellen and Tracy! It’s been such a joy to see your designs—you are both incredible artists.

And we’ll be back tomorrow for another round of winter containers. Hopefully you’ve spotted something you’d like to try or re-create in your own containers this season!

Exclusive: Nurx And Journey Clinical Co-Founders Raise $6M For Mental Health Startup Siggy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Siggy has raised $6 million in pre-seed funding to build a mental health service that uses AI to collect patients’ histories and track progress between appointments with licensed clinicians, the startup told Crunchbase News exclusively.

The company brings together two founders who previously built businesses that help patients get treatment outside a traditional doctor’s office. Jonathan Sabbagh co-founded Journey Clinical, which supports practices offering ketamine-assisted psychotherapy, and Edvard Engesæth co-founded Nurx, which provides birth control and other healthcare services online.

Village Global led the financing for New York-based Siggy. Notably, several investors in the round previously backed one or both founders. They include Albert Wenger of Union Square Ventures, Mercury co-founder Immad Akhund, and Cityblock Health co-founder Toyin Ajayi, through Coalition Operators. Other participants include PsyMed Ventures, Mystic Ventures and Gokul Rajaram.

The founders said they began building Siggy in late 2025. The round, which they plan to use to hire more clinicians and engineers, develop the software and work on regulatory requirements,

is the first outside funding for the company. The startup recently began accepting paying patients ahead of its public launch, and the founders said they next plan to test the service with paying patients before expanding further.

Sabbagh, Siggy’s CEO, told Crunchbase News in an interview that the company is addressing a problem he encountered in mental healthcare. Therapists and the clinicians prescribing their patients’ medication often work separately. Patients may see a therapist weekly but have only a brief appointment every few months with their prescriber. This leaves little time or opportunity to discuss changes in symptoms or medication side effects.

To address that problem, Siggy uses AI conversations and daily check-ins to gather information clinicians can review between visits.

“AI helps maintain the conversation and organize what patients share, so clinicians have a richer picture of the person that they’re treating,” said Engesæth, the startup’s COO, in an interview.

Patients begin by answering questions about their history through a free AI conversation. They then meet a licensed clinician by video, who reviews that information. The clinician then makes a diagnosis and prescribes medication if appropriate.

Patients can talk with Siggy’s AI between appointments and can also check in each day to report their sleep, mood, medication and how they feel physically. The company combines those answers with data from connected wearable devices to produce a daily “Siggy Score” out of 100. The score is visible to both the patient and clinician.

Patients can opt to connect wearables such as Whoop, Oura or Apple Watch, adding sleep, activity, heart rate and heart rate variability data to the daily check-ins that underpin the Siggy Score. The company aims to use those patterns to flag difficult stretches earlier, including when patients report feeling fine despite worsening sleep or other physical changes.

If the score changes, the software flags it for a clinician to review. Patients also receive weekly check-ins during their first eight weeks of treatment, according to the company.

Clinicians decide whether to continue treatment or adjust medication, and also handle all medical questions. When appropriate, they can help patients taper off gradually. Siggy does not prescribe controlled substances and is not a crisis service, the founders said.

One important note: Patients can continue seeing their existing therapists while using Siggy, Sabbagh said. A planned feature would let patients share information with other providers and involve loved ones in their care.

As with many startup founders, Sabbagh’s interest in mental health treatment grew out of his own experience. After two decades in finance, he experienced burnout and was eventually diagnosed with PTSD related to childhood trauma. He said ketamine-assisted therapy helped him recover, leading him to return to school to study psychology at 38.

He subsequently co-founded Journey Clinical. Sabbagh said he has since become its executive chairman, remaining involved while taking on the CEO role at Siggy.

Engesæth, a physician by training, was an early investor in Journey and advised Sabbagh as he built the company.

Before that, Engesæth co-founded Nurx, which initially helped patients obtain birth control prescriptions and receive medication at home. He said the company grew to more than 2 million patients and an annual revenue run rate exceeding $100 million before merging with Thirty Madison in 2022.

The two had considered starting a company together for years. With Siggy, they say they want to make the regular contact and coordination Sabbagh had with his own providers available to more patients.

Siggy accepts commercial insurance in all 50 states and is expanding into government insurance programs. Patients can also pay directly; insured patients’ costs vary by plan.

Insurance was a priority because many prospective patients cannot afford treatment out of pocket, Engesæth said. The founders also say Siggy’s service will be permanently free to veterans.

Amrit Rao, a general partner at Village Global, told Crunchbase News via email that the founders’ experience building healthcare companies was a major reason he invested.

The fact that Siggy accepts insurance was important too. “Pairing that vision with an insurance-based model makes the ambition about access as much as technology,” he said.

Mercury co-founder and CEO Immad Akhund, who invested personally, is a repeat backer of both founders.  He pointed to their longer-term ambition of using AI to understand each patient’s brain health well enough to detect changes early.

“That’s why I’m backing them again with Siggy,” he added, “and why I’m excited to be in early on what they build next.”

Hubble Learns Galactic History From Stars

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This NASA/ESA Hubble Space Telescope image provides a lesson in galactic history. The sparkling collection of stars is named Messier 30 (M30) after the French astronomer, Charles Messier, who cataloged M30 and other diffuse objects that might be mistaken for comets in the early telescopes of Messier’s time. M30 lies about 27,500 light-years away in the constellation Capricornus.

M30 is a globular cluster: a roughly spherical grouping of stars containing anywhere from tens of thousands to millions of stars. These clusters generally reside on the outskirts of galaxies, orbiting within a vast, diffuse region called the halo like a swarm of moths around a lightbulb. Our Milky Way galaxy has at least 150 globular clusters, the nearest of which is about 6,000 light-years away. These clusters are impressively ancient: astronomers estimate their ages are between 11.5 to 13.5 billion years, making certain globular clusters nearly as old as the universe itself.

Globular clusters have long played an important role in helping us learn about our galaxy. More than a century ago, astronomer Harlow Shapley used observations of a particular type of variable star to measure the locations of and distances to dozens of globular clusters within our galaxy. His work showed that the Sun is not in the center of our galaxy, and that the galactic center lies toward the constellation Sagittarius. (His distance measurements were incorrect, however, and he concluded that our galaxy is about twice as large as it really is.)

Today, astronomers continue to learn about our galaxy by observing globular clusters. Because globular clusters are so ancient, they can give us clues about how our galaxy developed long ago. Globular clusters that formed within our galaxy can act as time capsules, preserving the original chemistry of our young galaxy.

However, it’s possible that not all globular clusters that orbit within our galaxy today formed there. Researchers have discovered that M30 and many other globular clusters orbit in the direction opposite from the way our galaxy spins. This may mean that these clusters did not form within our galaxy at all. Instead, our Milky Way galaxy may have captured them in the distant past!

Hubble has observed M30 multiple times. A previously released image of M30 featured data from Hubble’s Advanced Camera for Surveys (ACS) and was part of an observing program (#10775; PI: Sarajedini) that surveyed dozens of Milky Way globular clusters in red and near-infrared light, revealing stars down to just 20% of the mass of the Sun. A more recent program (#13297; PI: Piotto) returned to M30 and other globular clusters in our galaxy, this time observing the clusters with Hubble’s Wide Field Camera 3 and using three filters that captured bluer light. This program collected data that was critical for studying exotic objects like “blue straggler” stars as well as for investigating the multiple populations of stars that can inhabit a single globular cluster.

More recently, Hubble’s ACS viewed many of these globular clusters again. Those observations were part of a program (#16298, PI: Libralato) that looked at how different populations of stars in globular clusters moved over the 15 years since Hubble first observed them — a feat made possible by Hubble’s longevity and ability to discern fantastically small details in distant objects.

The image above was created with data from all three observing programs and includes the wide range of wavelengths Hubble can see — ultraviolet, visible, and near-infrared light. Each wavelength helps us better understand how these groups of stars formed and evolved.

Claire AndreoliNASA's Goddard Space Flight Center, Greenbelt, MDclaire.andreoli@nasa.gov

Since its 1990 launch, the Hubble Space Telescope has changed our fundamental understanding of the universe.

These jewels of the night sky offer us a glimpse at the lifecycle of stars.

Hubble's most notable scientific discoveries reflect the broad range of research and the breakthroughs it has achieved.

Explore Hubble's iconic images of the universe, and view photos of mission operations and astronauts servicing Hubble in space.

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

Taming Typography: Building a Modern CSS Reset

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

CSS can now make choices about type that used to sit outside a reset. text-wrap: pretty can prevent a stranded word, container query units can scale a heading to the column it occupies, and text-box can trim the half-leading built into a line box. Some of these features are experimental and browser support is uneven, so they need guardrails rather than blind inclusion.

I’ve put together tidy-typography.css, which gathers the useful parts into a default stylesheet: rendering and fallback rules, fluid sizing, vertical rhythm, wrapping, hyphenation, and OpenType detail.

Use it whole, or take the declaration that solves the particular irritation in front of you. I’ll walk you through all the details in this article and give you the full code at the end.

font-synthesis: none stops a browser from inventing bold or italic when the family lacks that face. The smoothing and text-rendering declarations are rendering hints, mainly for WebKit; results still vary by platform.

text-size-adjust disables mobile font inflation that can make a deliberately sized layout jump on rotation. font-size-adjust: from-font uses font metrics to reduce the apparent size change while a fallback swaps for a webfont.

(As with other snippets in this article, this ⁠:root block is a partial excerpt the final stylesheet combines it with additional custom properties defined later on.)

Viewport-based fluid type does not know how wide its element is. A heading in a narrow sidebar can therefore scale as though it fills the viewport.

container-type: inline-size makes the wrapper a container query, so its children can use cqi: a unit based on the container rather than the viewport. Replacing vw in clamp() lets the same heading scale appropriately in an article column or a 300px sidebar without component-specific media queries.

line-height includes space above the cap-height and below the baseline: half-leading. It becomes obvious when text must align tightly with another element. text-box-trim removes it, while text-box-edge measures from capital-letter caps to the alphabetic baseline. This is a metrics-aware alternative to font-specific negative-margin nudges.

Selectors Level 5 proposes :heading, matching h1 to h6 and [role="heading"]. It has not landed in browsers, so keep it behind an @supports selector() check.

Default heading wrapping can leave one short final line. text-wrap: balance distributes text across its lines and works best for blocks of up to about six lines. For paragraphs, text-wrap: pretty avoids a stranded final word without balancing the whole block. Both replace JavaScript orphan fixers. Editable text needs text-wrap: stable, which prevents each new word from reshuffling the paragraph.

hyphens: auto relies on the browser’s language dictionaries, so the document needs a correct lang attribute (for example ). Without one the browser has no dictionary to use and won’t be able to hyphenate text. Even with the correct lang, the defaults can leave tiny word fragments or several hyphenated lines in a row.

hyphenate-limit-chars controls the smallest breakable word and fragments; hyphenate-limit-lines prevents a ladder effect. hyphenate-limit-zone makes breaks less frequent, while hyphenate-limit-last protects the final word. Safari needs the prefixed equivalents, so declare both sets together.

Set code, variables, and headings to hyphens: manual: a broken code word can change meaning, and a hyphenated heading usually reads as an error.

Punctuation marks don’t carry the same visual weight as letters, so a quotation mark sitting at the start of a line can look slightly indented against the text below it. hanging-punctuation lets specified punctuation sit fractionally outside the text block’s edge.

The first value handles opening punctuation, and last handles closing punctuation at the end of a block, while allow-end lets punctuation hang past the right edge rather than force-wrapping. The result is a text block whose edges read as genuinely flush.

Underlines have a related problem. Browsers apply flat, one-size-fits-all thickness and position regardless of the font’s actual weight.

text-decoration-thickness: from-font reads the thickness the font’s designer intended. text-underline-offset gives descenders room to breathe, and text-decoration-skip-ink: auto interrupts the underline wherever a descender crosses it rather than drawing straight through the letter.

Variable fonts often include an optical size axis, or opsz. It lets a typeface use thinner strokes at large display sizes and thicker, more legible strokes when set small.

font-optical-sizing: auto lets the font make that adjustment based on its actual rendered size. The @supports block covers the older font-variation-settings syntax some browsers still need, while font-variant-ligatures and font-kerning enable the font’s own ligatures and kerning pairs.

Light text on a dark background genuinely reads heavier than the same weight on a light background. Where a font ships a GRAD, or grade, axis, the dark mode block dials that perceived weight down without changing glyph shape or width the way an actual weight change would. Fonts without a GRAD axis safely ignore the setting.

Mixing a monospace font into running prose often exposes a mismatched x-height at the same declared font-size. The size-adjust keyword, set once at the @font-face level, scales that font’s rendered output specifically, leaving the actual font-size value untouched.

Numbers in a table need identical widths so they stack into a clean column on the screen. Conversely, numbers in flowing text don’t need the same treatment and should sit naturally alongside the surrounding words.

The tabular-nums and lining-nums keywords give table digits a fixed width and full cap height. oldstyle-nums and proportional-nums let digits sit alongside lowercase letters without sticking up and disrupting the sentence’s texture.

font-variant-emoji makes the choice between full-colour and monochrome emoji explicit. Without it, the browser or platform gets to choose a fallback, and the result can vary more than you expect. Apply .emoji-color or .emoji-mono to containers where you want to enforce that choice, rather than setting it globally on the body.

Quote style and character order differ by language and region. English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention, and French uses guillemets with a thin space.

The quotes property declares the order for each language through :lang(). The native q element then inserts the right pair through open-quote and close-quote. The same markup can render correctly in each locale without hardcoding characters into the content.

Wrapping the rules in :where() keeps them at zero specificity, so a project can override the quote style with one plain class.

Default sub and sup styling is synthesized. The browser just shrinks the text and shifts the baseline, rather than using glyphs actually designed for that position.

font-variant-position switches to the font’s own purpose-built sub and superscript glyphs where they exist, resetting vertical-align, font-size, and line-height so they don’t fight against the real glyph’s intended size and position. Most typefaces only include proper numerals rather than letters, so check the specific font before applying this broadly.

:where() always resolves to zero specificity, which is useful for a reset. Every rule here is an opinionated default, and wrapping it in :where() lets a project override a property with a plain class.

:is() solves a different problem: repetition. The heading selector list appears several times in a typographic stylesheet, and grouping it avoids the risk of forgetting a level.

Unlike :where(), :is() carries the specificity of its most specific argument.

Here’s the complete reset, combining the stable layers. Unsupported declarations are ignored by the browser, so the stylesheet can be adopted gradually.

Much of this CSS removes work that used to leak into other parts of a build. text-wrap can take over orphan handling from JavaScript, container query units keep a component’s type scale tied to its own width, and text-box-trim avoids the negative margins once used to compensate for leading. quotes, font-variant-position, and font-optical-sizing hand several smaller details back to the language and the font instead of a collection of one-off fixes.

Browser support will continue to change, especially for text-wrap and text-box-trim. Unsupported declarations are ignored, but that only helps when the unenhanced typography is acceptable in its own right.

Some of these rules are still too new for a baseline reset. A news article, a marketing page, and an editable product interface do not ask the same things of their text; the set that belongs in yours depends on the font, the content, and the browsers you still need to support.

We've got lots of ways to help you connect with the greatest front-end community in all the land.

Sign up to get front-end roundups, browser updates, and exclusive content sent to your inbox bi-weekly.

"English nests single quotes inside double quotes, British English reverses that convention,"

"English nests double quotes inside single quotes, American English reverses that convention (like it usually does),"

I don’t understand the reason to use font-synthesis: none;. Isn’t synthesis just a fallback for when the author makes a mistake with the font faces or when the font file doesn’t load? Users’ fallback font-families likely have weight and style variants but probably not small-caps, maybe it at least should be allowed to be synthesized.

Instead of max-width: 65ch; being applied to p, li, dd, I’d consider applying it to p, dl, ol, ul. Limiting the width of the lists, rather than the list items, prevents longer items from “sticking out” relative to the paragraphs above and below, do to the items’ inline-start margins, padding.

The browser should automatically use French-style quotes (« ») within lang=fr; appears the British quote convention is not automatically used when lang=en-GB, at least not on my USian computer.

It might be worth noting more clearly that some of these are experimental/straight up don’t work today.

hanging-punctuation is Safari-only and doesn’t seem to support quotation marks in my testing, so the text is perhaps inaccurate on that point? Many of the hyphenation properties also only work in Safari, and require the prefixes described as “legacy” in the article.

It’s mentioned briefly in the intro that some stuff is experimental, but being more explicit in the sections about what does/doesn’t work currently would be great.

Definitely cool to explore the upcoming features, but feels a bit odd to frame it as a “reset” when it will likely change how text looks in the future in untested/unexpected ways, and includes superfluous code that won’t actually impact rendering today.

What Your Hydrangeas Need in November – 5 Ways to Get Plants Winter-Ready and Kickstart Your Best Blooms Ever

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Plant These 7 Flower Seeds in November for Gorgeous Early Blooms Next Year

What Your Christmas Cactus Needs in November – Ace These 4 Tasks for Fabulous Holiday Flowers

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Although hydrangea flowerheads can look pretty framed against a winter landscape, it’s important not to leave them to their own devices this month. Make a little time in November for these care essentials

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As November arrives, backyard activity may be slowing, but beneath the surface, hydrangeas are entering a critical transition. As day length shortens and the air gets fresher and nippier, deciduous shrubs shift their energy into the roots. Understanding exactly what to do (and not do) for hydrangeas now begins with recognizing that dormant plants are still susceptible to moisture stress and sub-zero cold.

Obviously, not all hydrangeas are the same. Some form buds on old wood, which means pruning now is a no-no. Other types are hardier than others, and so need less winter protection. So in order to give hydrangeas the best winter care, you must first know exactly which type of hydrangea you have. However, these November care tips can all help to make a big difference to next year’s display.

Taking action this month gives you enormous benefits in the approach to the colder months ahead. Taking time now can help you shield key dormant buds from bitter cold, adjust water levels, stabilize and protect roots, and ensure your plants emerge stronger than ever next spring. So don’t chance it and leave your favorite shrubs unattended this month. Here are the crucial tasks to do before winter…

Effective fall care lies primarily in knowing your hydrangea type and variety. Varieties that bloom on old wood, such as bigleaf hydrangea (H. macrophylla) and oakleaf hydrangea (H. quercifolia) have already formed embryonic flower buds. If these buds freeze or are pruned off, next year's blooms will be tragically lost before you see them. Conversely, panicles (H. paniculata) and smooth hydrangeas (H. arborescens) produce flowers on new wood grown in spring, making them more resilient to frosts.

Popular bigleaf cultivars such as Nikko Blue or the Cityline series and serrated mountain hydrangeas need bud protection in cold zones. Cold-hardy panicle varieties like Limelight or Bobo and native smooth hydrangeas like Annabelle rarely require insulation, though they still benefit from consistent root hydration.

November hydrangea care also depends on your location, climate and USDA hardiness zone. Gardeners in northern zones 3-5 experience frozen ground and drying winter winds, demanding comprehensive stem barriers and thick thermal mulches. In contrast, those in southern zones 7-9 must contend with erratic temperature swings, where unseasonal late-fall warm spells can trick dormant buds into opening prematurely, only to be sheared by subsequent frosts.

Soil type also matters. Heavy clay soils hold water and risk winter ice pockets around roots, while light, sandy soils drain rapidly and dry out under frozen winds. Before tackling these tasks, it’s worth investing in a soil diagnostic tool with a long probe. The XLUX Deep Soil Moisture Meter, available from Amazon, gives instant readings to make better decisions before the first hard freeze. Don’t forget your trusty bypass pruners to remove any dead or damaged branches safely, plus some isopropyl alcohol to clean blades before and after use. You’re then ready to start…

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

One of the kindest things you can do for your hydrangeas this month is to “think twice, cut once” when it comes to pruning. One of the biggest hydrangea mistakes is indiscriminately shearing back during fall cleanups. For old-wood bloomers like bigleaf, mountain, and oakleaf varieties, the buds that produce next summer’s flowers are already set near the tips of the stems. Pruning these in November removes those flower buds, reducing or eliminating next summer's blooms.

For these hydrangeas, you need to resist all structural shaping, topping, or cutting back until immediately after flowering finishes next year. The only exception is removing dead, diseased, or broken stems, which can be cut down to the base at any time. Use sharp bypass pruners like the Felco 2 Bypass Pruning Shears, available from Amazon, for exceptional clean cuts.

Equally important is leaving the faded, papery flower heads all winter long. While it might be tempting to tidy up dried flower clusters on varieties like Endless Summer or PeeGee, these old blooms act as shields for the tender buds tucked beneath them. So it’s a good idea to leave them in place now. Dried flower clusters also add visual interest to the winter garden, especially when they collect small mounds of snow. Then, in late winter or early spring, as fresh green growth emerges at the base, you can snip the old flower heads off above the first set of green buds.

Hydrangeas need plenty of water, even now. While deciduous foliage yellows and drops, hydrangea roots take up moisture until the soil drops below freezing. Winter air and winds can be drying, and with the ground frozen, the roots cannot replace what gets lost. So don’t be tempted to put your hoses and watering cans away too early. Entering winter in dehydrated soil can lead to brittle branches and dead tips by spring. Get your hydrangeas as hydrated as possible before winter really begins.

If you’re having a rainy fall, you might not need to water hydrangeas, but check the soil and give your hydrangeas a good soaking as needed. If rainfall is sparse, provide a deep, slow soaking every 10-14 days. Aim to moisten the soil 8-12 inches (10-15cm) deep, encouraging roots to settle deeper underground. Prioritize any newly planted shrubs, which need more water as they get established.

Soaker hoses are great options for targeted and controlled watering. You can buy Expert Gardener 50ft Soaker Hoses from Walmart to help you direct the flow wherever you grow your hydrangeas. Stop supplemental watering once morning frosts result in frozen soil.

Soil temperatures fluctuate erratically during late fall and early winter. Rapid freeze-and-thaw cycles cause frost heave, where expanding and contracting soil pushes shallow root crowns above the soil line, exposing roots to drying winds and freezing cold air. You can give key hydrangeas a helping hand by giving them extra insulation this month. This stabilizes soil temperatures, preserves deep moisture, and protects delicate roots through winter.

Start by adding a top-dressing of compost This will slowly break down over winter, releasing micronutrients that enhance soil and slowly feed the roots. A great composting option at this time of year is mushroom compost. You can get lush ready-made bags of Espoma Natural Mushroom Blend Fertilizer, from Walmart, to prime the earth and provide steady support through the colder months.

The compost can do double duty as an insulating mulch, but you can also use other types of mulch, like shredded bark or chopped leaves. Add a 3-4-inch (8-10cm) layer of coarse, loose mulch. I like Back to the Roots Organic Premium Wood Mulch, available from Amazon, to help lock in late-season soil moisture and regulate soil temperature. Always leave a 2-inch (5cm) gap around the woody stems to reduce the risks of fungal rot and gnawing rodents.

If you’re in zones 4-6 growing bigleaf or oakleaf hydrangeas, sub-zero cold and winter winds pose a big threat to next year's blooms. As noted, old wood hydrangeas have flower buds now. While the roots may be hardy to zone 5, flower buds can suffer frostbite below 0°F (-18°C). If stems die back, old-wood varieties will produce foliage during the summer, but zero flowers. So depending on your hardiness zones, consider adding some physical protection if your winters are very cold.

Do this in late November, after the plant drops its leaves and enters dormancy. Drive four sturdy hardwood or metal stakes into the ground around your hydrangea. Keep them several inches clear of the branches. Then wrap breathable fabric around the outside of the stakes to make a wind barrier. Use burlap or jute, such as Wellco Gardening Burlap Roll from The Home Depot, to protect against biting winds while maintaining healthy air flow around the plant. Secure with zip ties or twine.

For extreme northern climates, you can construct a cylinder of wire fencing around the plant and loosely fill with dry pine needles or oak leaves. Just avoid packing the leaves tightly and never use plastic tarps, which trap condensation and bake tender stems on sunnier winter afternoons.

Hydrangeas in outdoor pots, ceramic planters, and decorative troughs require proactive winter care. Unlike in-ground plants, potted roots are exposed to freezing air on all sides. An unprotected container's soil temperature can be 20°F lower than the garden soil, putting roots at risk of freezing and killing the plant outright. Ceramic and terracotta pots also absorb water, making them susceptible to cracking when wet soil freezes and expands. So you’ll need to give your container-based hydrangeas more protection if you have very cold winters.

Begin by grouping potted hydrangeas together along a sheltered, south or east-facing foundation wall, out of wind corridors. Elevate your pots off cold concrete using pot feet or bricks to ensure drainage holes remain clear. Wrap the body of each pot in bubble wrap, thermal burlap, or foam insulation, securing the wrap beneath the outer rim without covering the top soil. You can also pick up floating plant jackets or anti-frost fleece covers, such as the Valibe Shrub Protection Plant Jacket from Amazon, which you can slip over potted hydrangeas in cold snaps.

If you are in zone 6 or colder, move your potted hydrangeas into an unheated garage, garden shed, or cold frame for the winter. These sheltered spaces keep plants dormant while protecting them from sub-zero drafts. Check on them once a month, providing a splash of water so the roots never completely dry out.

As well as the things you should be doing this month, preparing your hydrangeas for the colder months requires knowing what to avoid. We’ve touched on pruning certain hydrangea types. Avoid aggressive pruning on old-wood varieties like bigleaf and oakleaf hydrangeas, which shears away next summer's flower buds. Another thing to avoid doing is allowing roots to dry out prior to ground freeze, as desiccated roots are vulnerable. Keep watering if the soil is dry and remains unfrozen.

Never apply high-nitrogen fertilizers at this time of year. Feeding now forces a flush of soft, tender growth that cannot harden off before freezing weather strikes, causing stem dieback. Don’t use clear plastic sheeting or non-breathable tarps as winter wraps, as they trap humidity, promote mold, and create greenhouse heat spikes on sunny days. This can trick dormant buds into waking prematurely. Finally, don’t just assume all hydrangeas need the same winter protection. Know your hydrangea type, how far north it is hardy, and how far you need to intervene to keep yours healthy.

Taking a little time in November to protect your hydrangeas makes all the difference for the year ahead. By maintaining hydration, shielding exposed old-wood buds from freezing gales, and insulating roots with a mulch blanket, you give your prized shrubs the exact winter protection they need. Come spring, your hydrangeas will emerge from dormancy strong, healthy, and ready to produce a summer display of blooms.

Need more ideas for getting the most from your plants, indoors and out, and looking for the best seasonal expert advice delivered straight to your inbox? Sign up for the free Gardening Know How Newsletter!

Exor pays in €60m of Juventus’ €250m raise, but UEFA deal limits January plans

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Juventus shareholders have cleared a capital increase of up to €250m, and majority owner Exor has already committed €60m of it. Giovanni Carnevali warned that the UEFA settlement means nothing extraordinary should be expected in January.

Juventus shareholders gave the board the power to raise up to €250m in new capital at the Allianz Stadium on 3 November. The vote came in the extraordinary part of the meeting and passed with 99.9% in favour. The same assembly approved the 2025/26 accounts.

Part of the money is already committed. Exor confirmed it would pay €60m straight away towards the capital increase. That payment supports the club’s equity and finances until the board uses its new authority. The majority owner has also guaranteed its own 65.4% stake in the raise and any shares other investors leave untaken.

The squad will not see the full sum. Shoring up the club’s finances across this season and next will take 45–50% of whatever is raised, according to Football Italia. That leaves roughly €100m mainly for first-team signings before the January window. Beyond keeping equity at a suitable level, the rest will go on controlling debt and keeping the team competitive, including transfers, Corriere dello Sport reports.

The accounts show why the balance sheet needs support. The consolidated loss for 2025/26 was €66.0m. That was €7.9m bigger than the previous year’s €58.1m. The result was in line with the forecasts in the club’s half-year report to 31 December 2025. The board had approved the accounts on 29 September. Another loss is expected in 2026/27, mainly because Juventus are in the Europa League rather than the Champions League.

The assembly was Giovanni Carnevali’s first as chief executive. He promised a serious approach to the January window. His warning was just as clear. As a serious company, Juventus must honour its settlement agreement with UEFA and does not expect to do anything extraordinary.

That agreement was announced on 30 June. It is a three-year settlement with the First Chamber of UEFA’s Club Financial Control Body. It relates to Juventus breaking UEFA’s Football Earnings Rule over the three seasons from 2022/23 to 2024/25.

For supporters, the message is mixed. The capital increase gives the club fresh resources. The settlement still dictates how quickly that money can be used on the pitch.

Shareholders also criticised the club for keeping a low profile in recent years. President Gianluca Ferrero declared Juventus open to dialogue with Tether.

The terms of the UEFA deal leave little margin. Missing the targets agreed with UEFA for the current season or the next two can lead to limits on the club’s UEFA List A and further sporting sanctions. The settlement runs until the 2027/28 financial year.

Valverde’s stoppage-time strike completes Real Madrid comeback at AEK for 12 points from 12

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Substitute captain Federico Valverde struck in stoppage time as Real Madrid twice came from behind to beat AEK Athens 3-2. The win keeps Mourinho's side perfect in the league phase and takes them past his qualification target, days before a trip to Valencia without Vinícius.

Federico Valverde came off the bench in Athens and settled a match Real Madrid had trailed twice. The captain’s long-range strike arrived in the second minute of stoppage time. It completed another away comeback in this Champions League campaign. Mourinho’s side remain perfect after four matchdays.

AEK struck first. Koita put the hosts ahead in the 12th minute. Bellingham had a goal ruled out for offside after a review just before the half-hour. He did not have to wait long. In the 32nd minute he equalised with a backheel from Cucurella’s cross.

AEK went ahead again with 15 minutes of normal time left. Stavros Pilios ran onto a pass from Koita and shot through Courtois’s legs. Madrid answered in less than three minutes. Denzel Dumfries played a low ball across goal and Mbappé made it 2-2.

The night ended badly for Koita. AEK’s opening scorer was sent off at 90+5, after the final whistle. He had kept protesting to the referee despite repeated warnings.

The winner carried extra weight given Valverde’s recent fitness. He felt strong discomfort in his right hamstring in the 23rd minute of the 2-1 Clásico win at Camp Nou. Mourinho said afterwards that Valverde had played more than 40 minutes injured. In Athens he started on the bench, yet still had the final say.

Madrid’s European record now reads four wins from four. They beat Inter 2-1 at home, came back to win 4-3 at Roma and beat Leipzig 4-1 at home before this trip. Their tally of 12 points takes them past the 11–12 point mark Mourinho set as the target for qualification. The next aim is a top-eight finish.

The pattern is notable. Both the Roma and AEK wins came after Madrid had fallen behind away from home. In Athens they had to respond on two separate occasions.

Attention now turns to LaLiga, where Madrid remain without Vinícius. On 16 October, the RFEF Appeals Committee rejected the club’s appeal and upheld his ban. Two games were for pulling Nathan Saliba’s hair against Villarreal. The other two were for confronting the referee afterwards.

Bellingham // Gyökeres // Aguilar // Valverde ⚽

Four special goals, which was the best? @Heineken | #UCLGOTD pic.twitter.com/KtPJGSRG8C

— UEFA Champions League (@ChampionsLeague) November 4, 2026

The suspension covered the league games against Sevilla, Barcelona and Racing. The last of the four matches is at Valencia’s Mestalla on 7 November, three days after the win in Athens.

Europe then returns. Madrid host PSV on Tuesday 24 November at 9pm CET. They then travel to Arsenal on Wednesday 9 December.
