Thomas: Given some of the things that have happened in Amazon recently where there has been agent accelerated SEVs, is there anything that we're learning about why agents can help us to make bad choices that we can put guardrails in, thinking more holistically in the way that we're building our guardrails and systems?

Luca Mezzalira: I don't have the full visibility on all the things that we are doing inside the engineering side, because obviously, as you can imagine, Amazon is a company with 1.8 million people. As you can imagine, it's difficult to have visibility on everything. That said, I think currently we are using heavily in general in our processes, agents. We are betting on AI heavily. We also developed Kiro as a fork of Visual Studio Code and CLI. We started to see, especially internally, a lot of tools bubbling around, built by engineers or by architects for helping us to increase the understanding of our business. In the latest few months, I've seen more tools than ever that started to bubble up in our npm repository of AI tools. The thing is, obviously, in order to operate properly, you need to have all your house in order. I have a few friends that were working in Meta and they explained to me how everything is automated, everything is well-suited.

I think Amazon grows organically. There are certain areas that work better than others. That's normal. Therefore, I think AI can augment or potentially replace certain activities better in this area that everything is well designed and oiled properly. While in other areas, it might struggle a bit because it will rely on patterns that are already implemented, learn from those patterns, and might not be the best patterns that you can find. For that, you cannot expect that you have the perfect outcome as you think. You need to first fix the issue, retrain, restart, and move on. This is good learnings for everyone, because the reality is not that AI will be, sprinkle magic everywhere and everything will be super amazing, unicorn everywhere. It will be an augmentation and it will help us to achieve more in a short amount of time. It can replace certain parts of our job. We need also to think that, again, if we provide wrong input, we are going to have a wrong output, inevitably. They cannot guess what we have in our head or what is intrinsically expressed inside the trenches.

Thomas: That neatly moves us on to the next block, which is, who's responsible? If we're putting these things out there, who ultimately is the person on the hook for all this code that's suddenly exploding into production?

Luca Mezzalira: Claude. We put Claude on call. Whatever is called Claude, it will be responsible. I strongly believe that we don't have to forget everything that we have done in the last 40 years in engineering. Especially in my experience in distributed systems, one of the key areas is that ownership is well defined. If you don't have clear ownership, then you don't expect that the system will work nicely. There are certain situations, like when I see large organizations implementing InnerSourcing that is basically open-source practices, but applying inside private repositories or projects where even there you have a team that is leading the project that accepts and revisits certain requests or discussions, like you were mentioning OpenClaw, for instance. I think those are not that are fading away. Now, the thing that I personally think about that is quite hilarious to a certain extent, that I think we will achieve more with the same amount of people or potentially with slightly less people, we achieve equal throughput of the past.

It seems like a classic circle in tech, because back in the days, when in frontend there was Flash, the same things that you could do in JavaScript with a team, we were doing with 1, 2 people max. With some performances that sometimes could result in a better thing, especially towards the end when you have access to GPU acceleration. We're talking about probably 15 years ago, if not more. That was quite fun for me seeing the after-Flash era where currently for doing something medium complex, you need like one or two teams for running that. That was quite hilarious. That's the thing that I draw from my experience.

James Hall: Who owns the code that they commit? It's got to be the engineer that commits it, you'd think. That causes a problem with AI fatigue, where you feel like you want to move really quickly, and you're opening PRs, getting colleagues to review, but you're having to review lots of other people's PRs as well. I think, I can't speak for everyone, but by the afternoon, you're full up now, that's enough reviews, you don't want to look at any more code. In a lot of organizations we've been working with, keeping the PR size really small has been extremely important. In fact, for some high-risk repos, 200 lines is the hard max, and that can be a little bit painful, but, for the right reasons, it's to make sure that everybody understands what's going on in the codebase. You've got someone else rubber stamping it. We use policy-bot, which is a GitHub project, quite widely, and we have team review and then risk review. It's tricky. The risk levels are going to be very different. If it's like a conference website, you might be able to just vibe the whole thing. It's probably ok, except for the registration form, maybe don't do that.

Danielle An: What I observe a lot nowadays is that engineers are not actually reading the code at all. When they check in, the other engineer also didn't want to read it, so they just all assume if it has passed, I just move on. Of course, like you said, that's ok for maybe QCon Conference, but that's not quite ok for a lot of things, like Meta, for example, does. I'm curious how you guys are enforcing that. It just seems impossible to enforce. I just see engineers fly by diffs like 20 a day, like nobody read this. It's like thousands of lines. How are you dealing with that?

Ivan Zarea: More specifically in our organization, there's a couple of things. On one hand, we also have helper bots that help review things. Also, we're investing in cross-service knowledge context that allows for bots to review things in a way that is context aware of how it affects other things. It's like a review with larger context, more like an architectural review. Finally, we're doubling down on in-person whiteboarding. We are a distributed company, so that makes it very difficult for us to meet. We do see that right now the speed of shipping has increased. That means that we need to be even more focused about drawing boxes and arrows and more disciplined about that. That is where the review and the brainstorming and the magic happens. Then the code implementation is a byproduct of that.

James Hall: I'd agree with that. Something else that we found quite useful is getting different models to do the reviewing than the models used to write them. Gemini for review, let's say, and Claude to write it. Gemini, we would write a file for it, which is based on the last hundred or last thousand PRs that have been merged. You can get Claude to just go and grab all the comments and all the nits, each team have got their own style, and try and encode that as well as possible into the review guidelines. Then when someone's reviewing it, it looks and feels like that project. It doesn't look like it was written by Claude.

James Hall: Only for review time. Maybe it doesn't.

Ivan Zarea: If you've ever had a conversation between a Gemini and a Libra, then you would understand that they do not work well together.

Thomas: It's interesting, I guess to a point you made earlier, Luca, about that we shouldn't forget about the things that have happened in the past. I think the DORA reports from the last couple of years just reinforced that. We see now that, yes, there's greater throughput, but the size of the batch is bigger. We know from many years of research that that leads to poorer engineering outcomes. It comes back to that tension again between the engineers may have had this training and this experience. There may be a new batch of engineers that don't get that experience, which is partly what this Anthropic report around debugging skills reducing in more junior engineers because they're more AI friendly. Also, the whole cohort that we've talked about before about people who are not engineers at all don't identify as that. There's a big drop in people that self-identify as a developer now deploying to the platforms. I think there's this tension there that we still need to find some way to resolve, but it isn't clear to me what that path forward is.

Luca Mezzalira: Personally, I have a mental model usually applied with every enterprise customer that I follow. Again, I'm working mainly with enterprises, so take with a pinch of salt. If you're working at a startup, the practice could be different. In my case, usually is a matter of understanding the team system and then understanding the socio-technical system. Plus dividing the system in subdomains and categorize them. In domain-driven design, there is this idea of core, generic, and supporting domain that you can assign characteristics to. At my previous company, when I was working in DAZN, that is a live sports streaming platform, we categorize the whole system in these subdomains and we assign capabilities. We assign a certain seniority of developers based on the domain that they were working on. These things help us also to define these practices. Because if I'm thinking in a generic domain or in a supporting domain, very often you buy more than building because are less important.

If you think about payment for Netflix, nowadays they build their own payment gateway, but in the past they didn't. They were just aggregating PayPal, Stripe, and all the others. This is integration. The type of engineering practices they apply and the way you use AI could be heavy. While if you are building on the heuristics of the video player, that basically for a company like Netflix is the most important thing, because it's the reason why it exists, there you need to be careful because you cannot have heuristics that are changed randomly inside the video player because it could affect the experience for the user. As you can see now, if you start to segment your software into subdomains and apply different characteristics and approaches, then you start to have high throughput in certain areas and maybe you are more careful in others. If you replay exactly the same example in banks, usually that's what happens. You start to identify certain areas that are more critical and you apply different techniques compared to others that are more lightweight.

Ivan Zarea: We do have a bunch of things here breaking in practice due to AI-generated code, and I'm curious, you mentioned that AI can cross-review itself or a different agent can review itself. Are there any practices like that or emerging trends that you notice with regards to security or like a red team, blue team type of thing? What are your thoughts and what do you notice with regards to security and securing the tooling that we build?

James Hall: AI is quite dramatically bad for security because the hackers are using AI and also people on the other side are using AI to generate quick prototypes using like Supabase. When AI gets stuck, the first thing it does is switch off the security problem. Like row-level security on Supabase or something, like it'll just flip that off immediately. That's where hackers come in and they're using AI to quickly blast lots of endpoints because people that are new to building apps, they don't realize that let's encrypt certificates, broadcast as a transparency log, and as soon as you publish them into the internet with an SSL certificate, hackers are at it already. Bots are trying to poke it, prod it, all the rest of it. LLMs can very quickly see if they can go sideways and do other nasty things across your infrastructure. Yes, it's like gasoline on the fire, isn't it? It's both ends. I don't know if this is a good fix. Claude reckon they've got a new security tool coming out. I don't know if that'll be any good.

Luca Mezzalira: We don't have to forget what we have done so far. It seems, apart from the hype, there's this idea where LLMs are in the center of our life now. I think we need to treat them as a tool more often than not at the moment. If I think of any other revolution that happened in software, we always treated the new things as a tool. It was part of an architecture. It was part of a software development life cycle. It wasn't the center of everything. That means we built in the past fantastic tools that enables through different techniques to test the software end-to-end on security side. Now, are they perfect? Maybe not. Is AI perfect? No. We know upfront. If we are capable to combine the two things and not creating a mutual exclusive decision, like we want to use only AI for doing everything, I think we will land in a very nice place, because now one tool that is deterministic majority of the time because they do static analysis, abstract syntax tree, they do a bunch of checks around the system, you will be able to have the best of both worlds.

I personally believe that in certain areas, despite currently if they are good or not, there is still the need of a deterministic output, that we like it or not. Even on UI, for instance, I strongly believe that there are areas that can be probabilistic, other areas that shouldn't be probabilistic. Some other things, in my opinion, is that we need to look holistically again on the software development life cycle and identify what we accept that could be a drift, that is not the ideal outcome that we expect. Others instead that we say, hang on, we want to use SonarQube, or we want to make sure that static analysis and cyclomatic complexity reaches a certain point if we still care about code, because that's another good question that was raised during this conference in several ways. I believe that there are still the tools that are still viable and we shouldn't ditch all in one go, in favor of AI.

Thomas: Who has done the thing where they say to Claude or your favored tool of choice, scan all of the network in my house and find the things that have got open endpoints so I can automate them, like my fridge? I think that is both fascinating and absolutely petrifying for me. I think the security question is a really big one.

I just want to touch on this because I think this came up earlier this year and it's a really interesting juxtaposition. This particular example is Tailwind, where you've got Tailwind is more popular than ever because it seems to be like React, a default of agents, and it's shooting through the ceiling of how many downloads it's getting per month. AI has completely obliterated their business model. What's going to happen in this world where the thing that funds the things we rely on removes the ability to fund it? I think this is quite interesting as well from a games economy point of view, where lots of people now have access to things where previously big studios only had the monopoly on it. What do you think about this?

Danielle An: Actually, one of the main things we have is, again, we have artists, we have game designers here, is that in the past, I think artists and game designers were blocked by what an engineer can write in code such that people can experience that thing, whatever the game is. Then, vice versa. Now we can all vibe code in a couple days. We all did. Actually, you guys are going to see live demos that's all runtime, nothing is baked. When you can do that, it's very interesting. Should we get money when we publish onto some social platform and a million people played it? The cost probably was clawed in $200 per person, 5 people, like $1,000. Whereas in the past, even just to ship something like that, you need so many cycles of engineering time, it easily would be 10 times more expensive or even more than that.

I think the bigger question here, I don't know if anybody actually has answers, is how the tokens between the creator of the content and the player of the content and the platform that hosts that content is going to scale up in a way where they all want to do it? If creators don't want to do it because they're not making money, it's going to burn the whole economy. If the platform is like, you guys are all having fun, but I'm not making money, so it's also not going to work. I think that dynamic just entirely shifts from there's a budget as if I'm making a movie and it could be $200 million that I already got for my game. It takes 18 months. It can make or break my studio. To like, ok, so we have cool ideas and it may only take $500 to prompt that. Then, how do I distribute that? How do I get this content to be ranked very high and discovered? How do I get to the right audience? I think it's going to be very disruptive, but in a very exciting way. I don't think there's any answers yet.

Thomas: What do we think about the Tailwind model?

James Hall: You may not know, but our head of frontend left us to be employee number one at Tailwind back in the day. Brad worked on the Tailwind VS Code extension while he was working at Parallax, and went over to there to build out their premium offering. I think it's super interesting. I was never able to monetize any of my open-source stuff, even though it's super popular, because I couldn't figure out a way to hamper it to then upsell something else. I think it was always on tenuous ground. You relied on people going to the website, going, I like this pack of this is how you use Tailwind for these problems, and I'm going to drop $59 or whatever, it's inconsequential for a big company. You get enough of those, and then you can build a team. That's what the Tailwind guys were doing, and gals.

I think they did very well, but I think they were in a quite precarious situation. I do think open source, that classic XKCD diagram, isn't it? It's like there's some thankless maintainer just maintaining one lib that holds up the whole thing. I don't know if I've got a good solution. I don't think it's going to get any better. It's certainly getting a lot worse. I'm seeing lots of contributions to open-source projects that are literally just vibe coded by OpenClaw or whatever, and a lot of pull requests on jsPDF and others that are just automatically closed, because it's just like, this is just rubbish. This GitHub open by default, anyone can open a PR thing, they're going to have to switch that at some point, because it's too much. Open-source maintainers have been ignored and underappreciated for a long time, and I think this has just accelerated that.

Participant 1: Do you think we will reach a point where the sheer quantity of code that's been generated and just repeatedly over and over again, that the core understanding is just lost? In terms of like nitpicky kind of, this problem P is here, that that is just completely absent. We have to lean on, ok, I need a general thing to understand this. Like, we all know that one engineer that you give them a bug and they're like, "Yes, I know what file that's in. It's here. I'm going to change that, done." Do you think that that is going to disappear?

James Hall: I think you do find when you're using AI, you almost feel like you're losing your abilities a little bit. I don't know if anyone else feels like that. I do think that muscle memory and being able to remember functions and how things stitch together does go away a bit. It goes to your point earlier about whiteboarding and having a clear picture. I think you just need to take yourself away from a computer, draw some boxes and have a walk, get some fresh air. I think hopefully it doesn't go away, but I could probably see a world where it does.

Thomas: Maybe I can offer a counter point to that slightly. I've had a very similar feeling about owning codebases and knowing about code and being the expert on it for a long time. Then I went to Meta where there is so much more code than I could ever imagine. I did try for a brief moment to understand it all, but it's not going to ever happen. I think people have to adopt a more large codebase mindset where you're never going to be the expert on everything, but you have to be the expert on finding the information. It's about adapting your techniques to identify and triage and then hone in on something, which again, going back a step is quite worrying to see the debugging trends going backwards. At some point you reach a point where you can't understand everything anyway, whether you've got AI or not. I think those techniques are really critical skills to develop in the future.

Ivan Zarea: I think this part of the craft is going to die out eventually. I see this trend actually somewhere in a different place. I see this trend in like support organizations, oftentimes where I've talked to customers and I see like, how did their support organizations operate? What is interesting is that, a lot of the time, support organizations were optimizing for solving numbers of tickets. With an increased influx of tickets, you cannot humanly sift through them. Then the natural answer is, ok, hire more support people. That actually decreases the quality of your support because then you have like endbrain sifting through this. Then it's a challenge to transition to a higher level where you're not looking at an individual ticket or an individual request and are using data to sift through trends and understand what's happening. I think that this is what we're expecting in our industry as well, where that part of the craft is going to slowly die out in certain areas like FFmpeg, like that XKCD comic that you mentioned. There are certain areas in which that is still going to continue. As a general rule for most of us, it's probably going to die out.

InfoQ Homepage News Inkitt Builds a One-Shot PR Creator with Resumable Agent Orchestration

Nov 04, 2026 2 min read

Inkitt has built a one-shot pull request (PR) creator that automates small, low-risk software changes, from task intake to a draft PR ready for testing and review. Triggered through Slack, Linear, or an API call from another agent, the workflow runs on remote infrastructure, reducing the need for developers to set up and supervise coding agents locally. Inkitt reported that the system was shipping code daily in a technical article published by Inkitt Tech.

The Agi Letkiewicz Tanner Lead Engineer at Inkitt, described it as

Event-driven orchestrator that coordinates short-lived agent containers rather than relying on a fixed pipeline.

Individual stages handle specification, implementation, verification, CI failure repair, and merge conflict resolution. The orchestrator persists each run in a database, allowing execution to resume from its recorded state.

Inkitt’s one-shot PR creator architecture (Source: Inkitt Blog Post)

The orchestrator separates decision-making from side effects. Its core transition function maps the current run state and incoming event to the next action without directly accessing a database, network, or language model. The orchestrator retains credentials for external systems, while agent containers receive short-lived repository tokens and model credentials. They cannot directly access Slack or Linear credentials, cloud identities, or arbitrary network connections. A separate check inspects the actual Git diff to ensure changes do not touch restricted paths.

Inkitt limits automation to changes within predefined boundaries. The workflow rejects tasks involving database migrations, schema or shared contract changes, CI and container build configuration, authentication, payment code, or package additions and updates. Verification, CI repair, and merge conflict resolution each have a maximum of two attempts. Duplicate webhook deliveries are handled through deduplication and conditional database writes, applying the principle of idempotent operations to workflows that may retry or receive repeated events. For CI completion, the orchestrator queries GitHub to determine whether all relevant jobs have finished instead of treating every job event as completion of the entire workflow.

Tanner wrote, describing the implementation challenges around idempotency, locking, retry budgets, and permissions.

The design shares some safeguards with other coding agents but uses a different orchestration model. GitHub Copilot’s cloud agent also restricts agent permissions and requires human review before merging, while SWE-agent focuses on solving repository issues through a model-driven interface for browsing, editing, and executing code. Inkitt’s account emphasizes persistent workflow state, bounded retries, and centralized control of external actions.

Human approval remains part of Inkitt’s process. The system opens draft PRs, cannot bypass branch protection or merge its own changes, and notifies requesters when CI passes and the changes reach staging. A person must mark a PR ready for review and manage reviewer requests and approval. For larger tasks, Inkitt also maintains a local version that keeps a human involved in the development process.

Phishing-Resistant Isn't Enough; Yubico Takes Aim at Identity-Verification Gap

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

News, news analysis, and commentary on the latest trends in cybersecurity technology.

The new YubiID service bridges identity verification with credential enrollment and recovery across existing identity platforms, closing a phishing-resistant authentication gap. Plus: post-quantum YubiKey beta.

Yubico wants to help employers verify a person's identity before enrolling login credentials — and again when the employee needs to regain access after losing a key or mobile device.

On Tuesday, the maker of YubiKey authentication devices unveiled YubiID, a subscription service entering limited early access that links identity verification with credential enrollment and account recovery. Announced at Yubico's inaugural YubiNation user conference in San Francisco this week, the service integrates with major identity providers, including Microsoft Entra, Okta, and Ping, and supports enrolling credentials on YubiKeys and mobile phones. This extends Yubico's reach to organizations that have decided against deploying hardware keys.

The launch addresses a growing recognition that protecting a login to be phishing-resistant is no longer enough. It does not establish that the right person received the credential or that someone requesting a replacement is the legitimate owner. Yubico field CTO Derek Hanson described that shift at the FIDO Alliance's Authenticate conference last month, saying the help desk "has moved from an operational control to an actual security control in most enterprises."

Related:Cyera's Oasis Security Buy Is All About AI Agent Control

Yubico is working with a group of unnamed "large organizations" as design partners and hopes to make YubiID generally available in the first half of 2027, CEO Jerrod Chong tells Dark Reading. The company has not revealed pricing.

According to Chong, the problem YubiID addresses used to be an afterthought: confirming that a key reaches the right person and that someone reporting it as lost is that person. The new service provides a baseline identity-verification capability, but customers can also use their own verification process or rely on other providers. Planned integrations with Clear, Nametag, and HYPR will allow organizations to combine providers whose coverage differs by region.

For most enterprises, this is a familiar scenario: Employees prove their identities to HR in one part of the office, then collect their login credentials from IT in another part. Connecting these steps reduces opportunities for impersonation because identity verification is required for both initial enrollment and enrollment of replacement credentials.

"Recovery is such a weak spot for credential management," Andrew Shikiar, executive director and CEO of the FIDO Alliance, tells Dark Reading.

Digital identity credentials provide enterprises with a way to reestablish a user's identity during recovery without relying on knowledge-based secrets, he says. Solving the recovery challenge involves more than just technology; it also requires policy, regulatory alignment, user behavior change, and business process design.

Related:USA Fencing Lunges Into the Hidden Identity Challenge in Amateur Sports

For organizations already investing in Okta, Ping, or Microsoft Entra, YubiID coordinates verification and enrollment; it does not replace those identity platforms. Yubico has built connectors to enroll credentials from those providers and also supports homegrown identity systems.

"We're not being an IDP," Chong says. "I think that there are more opportunities for collaboration than competition."

The practical benefit is tighter coordination. An organization can connect its chosen verification process to enrollment in the identity systems employees already use, rather than managing those steps as disconnected workflows. That becomes especially relevant for enterprises running multiple identity providers, where the employee's immediate need is simply to receive working credentials, regardless of which platform manages the account.

For a company onboarding 10,000 temporary workers ahead of the holiday season, YubiID can support both administrator-assisted, in-person enrollment and remote enrollment and verification. The benefit of giving the company multiple ways to connect identity checks to access setup, Chong says, is as much operational as security-related — fewer disconnected steps between verifying a worker and getting that person authenticated — though he did not provide figures on potential time savings or reductions in help-desk work. Interest is strongest among organizations with large, distributed, or seasonal workforces, including transportation, retail, healthcare, consulting, and global services companies.

Related:Flaws in Passkey Implementation Show Old Attacks Still Work

Yubico is entering a market where identity providers and verification specialists already offer overlapping onboarding and recovery capabilities. Microsoft Entra's verified account recovery became generally available in May, using identity verification to help users regain access and register authentication methods without relying on a conventional manual help-desk process. Nametag and HYPR — both planned YubiID integration partners — also sell workforce verification and recovery capabilities. Beyond Identity already connects Nametag's identity checks to passkey enrollment and recovery, while 1Kosmos combines identity verification with passwordless authentication.

YubiID's differentiator is connecting verification and enrollment across platforms, rather than tying the workflow to a single identity provider, Chong maintains. Yubico also brings its hardware business into those relationships: It makes the devices where many customers' authentication credentials reside. Chong connects that experience with the challenge of distributing keys, verifying recipients, and enrolling replacements across a global workforce.

Hardware is not required. Support for credentials on mobile devices could appeal to customers who find hardware key distribution too difficult. Those customers haven't necessarily rejected the keys' security benefits; rather, the logistics are "sometimes quite challenging," Chong says.

Federal agencies could also use employees' existing PIV credentials to establish their identities when enrolling FIDO credentials on YubiKeys or mobile authenticators, Chong says, while noting that those workflows are future potential capabilities and not fully available at launch.

Separately, Yubico announced a public beta program for a post-quantum-ready YubiKey. The beta YubiKey will support NIST-standardized post-quantum algorithms, though it did not specify which ones. Yubico has tested early implementations with Chrome and Firefox, with early keys sent to federal labs and hyperscalers. The company expects to submit the key for FIPS validation within about a year, depending on beta testing and customer feedback, although completion is not guaranteed.

Despite being "crypto-agile," Chong confirms that the beta devices cannot receive firmware upgrades, meaning organizations would later need to replace those keys if different algorithms are required.

"Doing firmware upgrades safely across the Web is not trivial," he says.

A June executive order directs agencies to transition high-value assets and high-impact systems to post-quantum cryptography for key establishment by the end of 2030 and digital signatures by the end of 2031. Organizations evaluating the beta keys will need to test both the new cryptography and the process for replacing and enrolling devices.

Yubico ultimately envisions providing verifiable proof that a person authorized a specific AI agent action, but Chong confirms that agent-authorization capabilities are not part of this launch. While the combination of Yubico's back-end services, applications, and hardware keys forms the foundation for additional use cases, for now the YubiID service focuses on verifying people, enrolling credentials, and handling recovery.

"In a simple way to look at it, we're offering an end-to-end solution rather than just a YubiKey solution," Chong says.

Focused on the constant pace of technological change, Jeff has covered the evolution of technologies, including professional broadcast, computing, networking, the Internet, mobile communications, modern cloud infrastructure and software development for over 30 years.

Before launching his freelance career, he was an editor at 1105 Media, where he served as editor-in-chief of Redmond magazine and contributed to its sister publications, including Redmond Channel Partner, Application Development Trends, and Virtualization Review.
